...basing all productions on the conflict of two contrary principles, grounding his disapproval on Grotius's <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645) was a Dutch jurist and theologian whose works were foundational to international law and Christian apologetics.</note> argument against the Manicheans <note>Followers of Manichaeism, a major dualistic religion that taught a cosmic struggle between a good, spiritual world of light and an evil, material world of darkness.</note>; that from two opposites, destruction might follow, but no growth <note>original: "augmentation"</note>.

But without struggle <note>original: "strife"</note>, no production arises; and without opposites, there is no struggle.

### What the two principles are when unified.

The two principles of light and darkness cannot be said to have a beginning, but are co-eternal. Yet one (the light) absorbs, or swallows up the other, just as the day does the night.

The *flagrat* <term>A term used by Jacob Boehme to describe a sudden flash, spiritual ignition, or "shriek" that marks the transition from one state of being to another.</term> or operation of the darkness is God's anger; and the *flagrat* or manifestation of the light is His principle of love. These two, when not separated, make one triumphant kingdom, working together <note>original: "wrestling"</note> to exalt the sublime joy of the holy, sweet, and divine will of God.

### The root of evil.

But after they were separated [by the apostasy <note>The abandonment or renunciation of a religious belief; in this context, Lucifer's rebellion against God.</note> of Lucifer], they became two worlds. That is, one is the dark, cold, fierce, harsh, bitter, sting-

The other is the delightful, loving, sweet, joyful, ravishing, holy world; yet the holy world has the power <note>original: "potence"</note> of the other as its perfectly perpetual root or fountain. Just as the natural heat in a man’s body is of such absolute necessity and use when it is moderated by a due proportion of cold (for that is the food of the heat), but if either heat or cold dominates, it becomes aggressively destructive <note>original: "rapaciously ruinous"</note>.

Now, while it was said above that darkness and light are without beginning and co-eternal, it is also true that angels, as well as all other creatures, had a beginning. Therefore, it may be asked: how can it be true, and in what sense should it be understood, that darkness should be without beginning, seeing that the Holy Spirit says,

—1 John 1:5.

—2 Corinthians 6:14.

<page-type>text</page-type>

### What the eternal darkness is not.

It is not meant to be a lack of knowledge <note>original: "nescience"</note>, a state of not knowing, a defect, or ignorance; much less is it an evil in itself.

### What is meant by eternal or original darkness.

Instead, it is understood as a shadowing <note>original: "adumbration"</note>, similar to a dark, raw state or lack of refinement <note>original: "crudity or indigestion"</note>. It is an eternal origin for substance, might, power <note>original: "potence"</note>, and essence. 

It is a center or root—harsh <note>original: "austere"</note> and serious—resembling a hot and cold fire. This principle is now understood to be the Father's specific attribute <note>original: "property"</note> or first principle; it is according to this that he is called a jealous (or zealous) God and a consuming fire. 

It is a world of power in its own right—secret and hidden (until the fall of Lucifer) within the second principle of glorious light and gracious love. It exists just as the night exists within the day, or as the wick of a candle exists within the brilliance <note>original: "splendor"</note> of its light. However, it is now revealed <note>original: "manifested"</note> and awakened—by the separation of the second principle from it—within or upon any separate, unclean spirit or creature.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

For although it existed from eternity, it was in the highest harmony. Now, after being severed from the love-principle, this is like a glowing fire whose flame has gone out; the dark life burns within 그 principle.

And it must be called the "first" principle because from it—as from an eternal root of living fire—the majestic splendor of glorious light is eternally generated. This light is the "second principle."

Thus, these two principles in perfect unity—as they exist in God in eternity—are the one and only highest, absolute, and complete good. However, wherever the light is withdrawn, there is found a wrathful, evil spirit, creature, thing, or world.

This raging spirit cannot deliver itself from the strong bonds of the constriction; and from that source, by the conflict, heat is excited, the extreme of which is fire.

## Chapter 2

### A description of the seven Fountain Spirits.

But before we consider the three worlds existing on the foundation of the two principles mentioned above, let the seven fountain spirits <note>original: "fountain spirits" — Translated from the German *Quellgeister*, these are the seven fundamental qualities or forces of nature in Jacob Boehme’s mystical system.</note> in the divine power of the Father be known. These are represented by the seven lamps before the throne; in the outward world by the seven planets; among us by the six days of the creation and the seventh day of rest; and also by the seven simple metals assigned <note>original: "appropriated"</note> to the seven planets; the seven stars in the Book of Revelation; along with many similar important and self-evident <note>original: "more than speaking"</note> demonstrations.

<summary>This page concludes a description of the elemental cycle of nature and introduces the "seven fountain spirits," which Boehme identifies as the core forces connecting the divine, the planetary, and the alchemical realms.</summary>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>Of Jacob Boehme's <note>original: "Behmen's"</note> Writings.</header>

A useful method for exploring this profound subject is:

1. To name, define, and describe each spirit separately and clearly.

2. To demonstrate them to human reason, and even to the physical eyes and senses. This provides assurance that the blessed Jacob Boehme was not just talking aimlessly <note>original: "beating the air"</note>, but was building on an unshakeable foundation.

The definitions of the seven fountain spirits <note>original: "fountain spirits" — Boehme's term for the seven primary active forces of nature and divinity.</note> within the divine power of God the Father are as follows:

**First:** The astringent, binding, piercing, compacting, and knitting power. This is a secret force hidden within the divine power of the Father’s *saliter* <term>original: "saliter" — A term Boehme adapted from "saltpeter" to describe the essential, energetic substance or "divine salt" of the spiritual realm.</term>. It is a sharp power, similar to salt. From this—within the corruptible and mortal realm—arises a restrictive power <note>original: "power locking up"</note>, like that found in physical earth and stones.

**Second:** An attraction that overcomes <note>original: "vanquishing"</note> the harsh astringency. This force is sweet, mild, and gentle, yet also bitter. It is the stirring of desire and the catalyst for life; it is also the source from which water is generated. From these two fountain spirits, the Heart or Son of God is generated from eternity to eternity. Also, from these (together with the rest of the seven spirits) is

<sig>F 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page defines the first two of Jacob Boehme’s seven "fountain spirits," describing the "astringent" force of contraction and the "attraction" that sparks life and generates the Son of God.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Boehme, fountain spirits, saliter, astringent power, attraction, divine generation, mysticism, alchemy</keywords>

**Third:** The bitter or "anguish" fountain spirit. This is the source of the mind, senses, and thoughts. In its positive state, it is a triumphant, exulting joy of the highest degree, even to the point of trembling within its own nature <note>original: "quality" — Boehme uses this word to describe the essential characteristic or "flavor" of a spiritual force.</note>. It forms all kinds of red colors within its own nature. In the "sweet" nature, it produces white and blue; in the "astringent" nature, it produces green, dark <note>original: "dusky"</note>, and mixed colors. 

However, if it is stirred up too much, it ignites the sweet and astringent forces, and everything becomes a raging, tearing, stinging, and burning poison, like the torment of a plague sore. When it is ignited in its hellish state <note>original: "property"</note>, it extinguishes the light and corrupts the sweet nature into a loathsome stench. It turns the astringent nature into a sharp, dark coldness, as well as a sour, foul, and bitter fierceness for all eternity. Yet, when it is ignited within heavenly power, it is the spirit of the jealous, zealous, and unquenchable wrath of God, as seen in Lucifer and his legions.

**Fourth:** Heat that produces light. This is the true beginning of life and of the true spirit of life. It ignites all the qualities and generates the light within them, from which arise the

senses and thoughts. For when the bitter spirit (accompanied by heat) passes through the astringent spirit and the sweet, mild, yielding passage, the two open gates of the eyes are created.

**Fifth:** Light-producing love. The holy, gracious love kindled by the heat within the sweet quality <note>original: "quality" — Boehme uses this word to describe the essential characteristic or "flavor" of a spiritual force.</note> is a completely pleasant, ravishing sweetness. It is light and glorious—the fountain of eternal, free, and immense brightness of love. Here, all tongues stammer, hands shake, pens blot, and hearts catch fire among those who taste, see, hear, and feel it. Even so, *Amen.*

**Sixth:** Sound or noise. This is the tone or sound of the Divine Word, from which comes speech and language, as well as the singing of angels and the opening of the divine kingdom of joy. Here, all colors, beauty, and decoration are seen. This tone—or *mercury* <note>original: "mercurius" — In Boehme’s alchemy-influenced theology, Mercury represents the "sound" or the moving, expressive power of the Word.</note>—originates in the hard astringency, which acts as the *Father*, while the whole *saliter* <note>original: "saliter" — Likely derived from saltpeter, this term refers to the divine substance or "soil" out of which all things are generated.</note> is the *Mother*. For if the astringency were both father and mother, a stone would make a sound.

**Seventh:** The body. This is generated out of the other six spirits, and they dwell within it as if in their Sabbath <note>original: "sabbath" — A state of rest and completion where the previous six forces find their end and fulfillment.</note>. In this body, all heavenly figures (joy, etc.) exist; it gives image and form to the spirit of nature, in which heaven itself and an-

angels are formed, and it is the mansion or body in which the other six spirits rest, like the kingdom of divine glory.

Now we can see that all the spirits together are God the Father.

### What the Three are in the Trinity

The life generated by all of them—and generating the life within all of them in triumph—is the true Son of God, the second Person in the Holy Trinity. And the power of the seven spirits, proceeding continually in the splendor of life and forming all things in the seventh nature-spirit <note>original: "nature spirit" — the manifested or "bodily" form of the spiritual forces</note>, is the true Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note>, the third Person in the Deity.

## Demonstrations of the first spirit

Having described the seven fountain spirits <note>original: "fountain spirits" — the fundamental energies or "qualities" that Jacob Boehme identifies as the source of all existence</note>, and now to follow the method proposed in the previous section, here follow the demonstrations of the seven in their proper order.

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>Of JACOB BOEHME'S <note>original: "Behmen's" — Jacob Boehme (1575–1624) was a German mystic and theologian whose work influenced later philosophy and Romanticism.</note> Writings.</header>

## Demonstrations of the first fountain spirit: astringency.

It should not be said that this or that spirit is "first" or "last," as they are all from eternity and exist within one another. However, that the astringency <note>original: "astringency" — in Boehme's system, this is the contractive force of nature that pulls things together to create solid form.</note>—which is called the first—is truly what it has been defined and described to be, will now be demonstrated.

All our outward senses inform us that there are cold, raw, tough, and impenetrable bodies, or a power tending toward impenetrability. Our reason agrees that there must be a root cause for this, and this root can only be called the "binding" or "astringency."

1. It is that which causes cold to condense air into water, and water into ice.

2. It drives the condensed residues <note>original: "feces" — here used in the alchemical sense to mean the solid remains or dregs of a substance.</note> of matter (with the help of the "bitter" quality) into earth or sand, and then further into stone.

3. It also compacts fluid parts into stalks, stems of grain, and so on, as well as the trunks, skins, and bark of trees.

4. It also encloses the tender brains and marrow within skulls and hollow bones.

<meta>catchword: 5. That</meta>

<summary>This page defines the first "fountain spirit" as "astringency," a contracting force responsible for the physical density of matter, from the freezing of water to the formation of bone and stone.</summary>

5. It contracts and hardens <note>original: "obdurates"</note> the subtle parts of metals into a solid form <note>original: "continuity"</note>, and restrains or confines their attractive powers <note>original: "allected virtues" — from the Latin *allectare*, meaning to entice or draw in</note>, even into oneness or a shared identity through that solidity. By these examples and a thousand more, the astringent spirit demonstrates itself to be a single source <note>original: "fountain"</note>, whose external representative—as acknowledged by both ancient and modern philosophy—is Saturn.

## CHAPTER IV.

### Demonstration of the second fountain spirit: Attraction.

We find something breaking these bonds; otherwise, the astringency <note>The contractive force described in the previous chapter.</note> would hold everything as if in a dark, dry, hard prison. Everything would be stone, bone, or similar lifeless solids <note>original: "concrets" — substances formed by the union of separate particles into a mass</note>; there would be no growth and no creation. Furthermore, the stronger this attractive power is, the stronger the other force binds; likewise, the tougher and heavier the astringency is, the more active and vigorous this second force becomes. Just as the more you compress air or water, the more violently that compressed body resists,

resists, just as wind causing earthquakes or gunpowder that is confined <note>original: "pent"</note>.

We find a contrast between sweetness and bitterness, which we clearly see originates from the cold astringency. Properly speaking, that astringency is a single thing and is the same for everyone. However, in its positive aspect—being its natural source—it is a sweetness; but in its raw, undigested state, it is a bitterness.

We find inclinations placed into things that are different from their own nature. Through this process, a person's harsh eagerness is (by the intervention of proper ingredients) trained and calmed into a friendly mildness. This new temperament becomes, over time, a natural habit, just as a bitter apple ripens into a sweet state <note>original: "temperature"</note>.

We find that this force provides constant opposition and interruption to the piercing astringency through its own bitter harshness. It is like the fire found within a stone; it is sparked by penetrating force <note>original: "violence"</note> and thus becomes the cause of life. The external instrument of this spirit is Mercury in conjunction with Jupiter.

# CHAPTER 5.

## Proofs of the third source-spirit: 
## Anguish.

This spirit is similar to (though only similar to) a dying, tightening <note>original: "astringent"</note> torment, like the sensations dying creatures feel, which our own senses often experience a foretaste of. Just as the second spirit is the cause of life, this third spirit is the cause of sensation, feeling, and perception. Consequently, it is the source of the mind and senses in all beings whom the Separator <note>The "Separator" is a term used in Boehme’s philosophy to describe the divine or natural force that distinguishes and organizes different qualities into specific forms.</note> has prepared and raised for that purpose, such as angels and humans, and to some degree in lower sentient creatures.

We find something that elevates the mind to great joy, even to an extreme of gladness. This cannot come from the first spirit, because that spirit depresses; nor can it come from the second, because that only provides life. Therefore, it must be this third spirit; for this spirit is an elevator to the highest joy and triumph, contributing to the peak of laughter.

We may find it not only in its moderate state, as mentioned above, but also in its extreme; for in every

## Chapter 7

### Demonstrations of the fifth fountain-spirit producing light and the love-fire.

This demonstrates itself in everything, more or less, on this side of the hellish principle. However, the first four (excluding this addition) are in, and in a manner compose, comprehend, and include the dark world: namely, the harsh astringency, strong attraction, raging anguish, and hungry fire. These limit the first or hellish principle, which can never comprehend the light; for that never proceeds to ignite <note>original: "enkindle"</note> it, for it lacks <note>original: "wants"</note> oil and must always lack it, because their perpetual enmity forever dries it up.

Now, that which establishes <note>original: "composes"</note> peace is where the fire proceeds to the fanning <note>original: "blowing up"</note> of light. The meekness of that light satisfies <note>original: "satiates"</note> the hungry fire and becomes food for the other three spirits, who before—like millstones without grain <note>original: "corn"</note>—grind and impetuously rub and grate against each other.

Daniel’s vision of four monarchies represents <note>original: "figures"</note> the four first forms <note>In Boehme's cosmology, the "seven forms of nature" are the fundamental forces of reality. The first four represent the "dark" or "fire" world before the light of divine love is kindled.</note>. All of these—excluding the fifth form, which is the kindling of the light—are called "four great beasts" (Daniel 7).

1.  The first was like a lion (verse 4); namely, the astringent binding <term>astringent binding: Boehme's first form of nature, representing contraction, darkness, and solidification.</term>, the mighty power of the lofty Babylon.

2.  The second was like a bear (verse 5); namely, the strong, cruel attraction <term>attraction: the second form of nature, representing the outward pull or expansion that creates motion.</term>, the inflexible, unalterable, and inexorable empire of the Medes and Persians.

3. And the Greek empire is said to have consisted of about a hundred and forty sorts of people or nations.

4.  The fourth beast was dreadful and terrible (verse 7); it was not likened to any specific beast, but (being like fire <note>Fire is the fourth form of nature, the "flash" or point of transformation where the dark forms are consumed and the light world begins.</note>) is said to devour and break in pieces (verse 13). Such were the Romans.

## Chapter VIII.

### Demonstration of the sixth fountain spirit: being the sound, tone, noise, or voice.

Is there any need to demonstrate this, which heaven and earth ring with? Even hell (according to its capacity) also has some impressions of it. It may be enough to briefly show that this is a distinct fountain <note>original: "fountain"; Boehme uses "fountains" or "source-spirits" to describe the fundamental forces that constitute existence.</note>. The fact that it is only the result of the united operation of the five preceding spirits meeting in the seventh is no argument against this also being a distinct one; for not one of them could exist without all the others. Astringency <term>Astringency: In Boehme's system, this is the first "form of nature," representing the harsh, contracting force of desire that "binds" or hardens energy into matter.</term> would have nothing to bind unless the matter were produced by the other properties; and the same is true for all the rest.

Just as the light is the opening <note>original: "opening"; here meaning a revelation or unfolding of something previously hidden.</note> of the darkness through the fourth fountain, the fire; so the voice arises from the first four fountains through the opening of the light. This reveals the kingdom of joy with its beauty, colors, and ornaments wherever it is treasured.

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: As</meta>

<summary>This page defines the "sixth fountain spirit" as Sound or Voice, explaining that while it arises from the interaction of the other spirits, it is a distinct property that reveals the "kingdom of joy."</summary>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>Considerations on the Scope</header>

Just as we see fire sleeping in a flint, awakened by a strike, so does a tone or sound spring up and spread itself as far as its power overcomes the resistance of opposing motions in solid objects, and then it expires.

Just as life lies in a faint within plants until it is revived by the spring, or as scents lie hidden in the dead earth until they are brought forth by the sun through the stalks of flowers: so the varieties of infinity remain secret and silent, as if in a mystery, until this eloquence <note>original: "oratory"</note> publishes them, and until this herald proclaims them. This herald, through its continuous motion, speaks all things and shows all things. The representative of this sixth source-spirit <note>original: "fountain spirit"</note> in the external world <term>outbirth: In Boehme’s philosophy, the "outbirth" is the physical or material manifestation of spiritual principles.</term> is Mercury <note>original: "Mercurius"</note>.

## Chapter 10

### Concerning the three principles of the Divine Essence <term>Divine Essence: the fundamental nature or being of God</term>

By a **principle**, we mean a primary originating power, a life that exists of itself, an original root or foundation—or in some sense, a mystery or *genus* <note>original: "genus"; a Latin term for a category or source from which things are produced</note> from which other spirits, bodies, species, or things originate as if from a supreme power.

In this sense, God is the only "one principle."

This one principle also consists of three eternal births or principles that never cease. They are constantly begetting, being begotten, and proceeding by, in, and from each other. All worlds, whether taken together or individually, are an image of these births, though they differ according to the "inscription" <note>the inner character or law written into them</note>, stamp, figure, and the degree of development they attain or are capable of receiving.

And although these three principles are joined by an unbreakable bond and are always inseparably one in God, they are nonetheless distinguishable from one another.

<summary>This page defines the concept of a "principle" in Jacob Boehme's philosophy and introduces the "three principles" of the Divine Essence. It explains that while God is a single unity, He manifests through three eternal, interactive births that serve as the blueprint for all created worlds.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Boehme, Divine Essence, three principles, eternal births, creation, spiritual philosophy, genus, God's nature</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 10 regarding the Divine Essence. It contrasts the unique status of humanity, created in God's image, with the rest of the created world, which reflects God's attributes in a more fragmented or "separate" manner.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

->**Considerations on the Scope**<-

[proper]ties <note>continued from the previous page: "properties"</note> [since the Fall].

But in other creatures, these separate properties are revealed in a terrible way. Although Adam was not meant to experience these within himself, it is something we now ought to understand.

God said, "Let us make man in our image, after our likeness" (Genesis 1:26). But He did not say this about the other creatures; in his original creation, man was the most perfect representation and offspring of God.

Yet every other creature—whether belonging to the astral <term>astral: relating to the stars or the celestial spirit that influences the physical world</term>, elementary <term>elementary: relating to the four physical elements of earth, water, air, and fire</term>, vegetative <term>vegetative: relating to the life-force of plants and growth</term>, or sensitive <term>sensitive: relating to animals and the capacity for sensation</term> realms, or even the dark world—represents Him in their various ways, some more perfectly than others. As it says in Romans 1:20: "For since the creation of the world, His invisible attributes are clearly seen, being understood through the things that are made, even His eternal power and Godhead."

And just as there are the united Three who testify in heaven, so also are there three that agree on earth: namely, the spirit, the water, and the blood <note>A reference to 1 John 5:7-8, used here to show how the earthly world reflects the triune nature of the Divine Essence</note>.

<summary>This page explores how the "separate properties" of nature are visible in all creatures, though man alone was created as a perfect image of God. It argues that all levels of existence—from the stars to the dark world—reflect God's eternal power and the triune nature of the Divine Essence.</summary>

## Chapter 11.

### Concerning the first principle, in four demonstrations.

The abyss <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "Abyss" or *Ungrund* refers to the groundless, irrational void that precedes all existence and differentiation.</note> of the Father! Distinct from the second and third principles, it is understood to be an eternal, indissoluble bond and the source of power <note>original: "original to potence"</note>, a center or root of substance. It is an earnest longing for the birth of the majestic light or brilliance; a harsh power like an eternal, infinite fire that is both hot and cold.

That this is so, and (to some extent) what it is, can be shown to the very senses.

### 1st demonstration of the first principle.

First, non-sentient <term>non-sentient: original "insensitive"; creatures or objects that lack the capacity for feeling or perception</term> creatures witness it. principle—their substantial root, center, basis, or immovable foundation.

Second, the elements are founded upon cold and heat, which represent this principle called "eternal darkness." From the various degrees of cold and hot fire, not only do earth and stones emerge, but also air and water.

Third, the astral world <term>astral world: the realm of the stars and planets, which Böhme believed shaped the material world and human character</term>—from which the forms of the elements spring—is rooted in the same darkness. This is most clearly seen in some of the "royal stars" called planets. Saturn’s condensing, strong, binding contraction imprints a dry, hard weight that is difficult to penetrate onto lead, stone, and similar materials. In the same way, Mars bestows these qualities on iron. Both planets are physical representations <note>original: "substantial figures"</note> of the same eternal principle.

# Of Jacob Böhme’s <note>original: "Behmen’s"</note> Writings.

> “Oh, that they were wise, that they understood this, that they would consider their latter end!” Deuteronomy 32:29. <note>The original text quotes a variation of this verse to emphasize the importance of understanding one's spiritual destiny.</note>

The **out-birth** <term>out-birth: in Böhme’s philosophy, this refers to the external, material world which was manifested out of the internal, spiritual principles</term> into which Adam’s fall has cast our mortal bodies is symbolized by the **earthen vessels**. Under the old religious laws, if these vessels touched anything unclean, they could not be purified but had to be broken; such is the mortal body of all the children of Adam <note>This refers to Leviticus 11:33. Böhme uses this to argue that the physical body, corrupted by sin, cannot be "cleaned" but must undergo the "breaking" of death to be transformed.</note>.

But those vessels which were made of brass or other metal became clean once they were washed. This is because metals are one step higher and more noble; they derive more directly from the **astral powers** <term>astral powers: the celestial influences and energies of the stars and planets that Böhme believed governed the material world</term> and possess a purer **tincture** <term>tincture: an alchemical term for a spiritual essence or quality that can transform and "color" a substance</term>. They are more simple and less **volatile** <note>original: "volatile" — here meaning unstable, prone to decay, or easily evaporated</note> than our **fleshly tabernacles** <note>fleshly tabernacles: a biblical metaphor for the human body as a temporary dwelling for the soul</note>.

## Considerations on the Scope

When Christ himself, in the flesh, preached salvation, his divine Wisdom—explained through the most familiar parables, the highest evidence, and more than angelic skill—was mocked and blasphemed by many. This mockery came not only from the lower, uneducated classes, but from the rabbis <note>original: "rabbies" — here referring to the Jewish religious leaders and scholars of Christ’s time.</note>; these were men skilled in the literal Law of God, men of parts <term>men of parts: an archaic expression meaning individuals of great talent, ability, or intelligence</term>, zealous and outwardly holy, the leaders of the people, and blameless according to the Law. 

However, the doctrine of the Lord Jesus was understood perfectly well by those (even if otherwise weak or simple) whose love for it resulted in obedience to it, and who, through the spirit of holiness, were taught to reject the pleasures of sin. To these people, no yoke was as easy as Christ’s, and no burden was as unbearable as that of sin. They knew what it meant to be "born again"—spiritually, and not by the will of the flesh. Christ’s life and doctrine were clear to them, and they remain clear to such people today, and to no one else.

If such people read these lines (having had their inward senses unlocked), they may find this **second principle** <term>second principle: in Böhme’s theology, this represents the divine world of light, love, and the Son of God, which stands in contrast to the "first principle" of darkness and fire</term> informing, enlightening, and assisting them, provided they pursue it with earnestness.

<meta>catchword: But</meta>

<summary>This page argues that spiritual wisdom is hidden from the intellectually elite and "outwardly holy" but revealed to the humble and obedient. It introduces the "second principle" as a source of enlightenment for those who are spiritually reborn.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, salvation, parables, rabbis, spiritual rebirth, second principle, divine wisdom, obedience</keywords>

This is because this principle strips them of their own self-will before it leads <note>original: "inducts"</note> them into the universal, holy will of God. And if divine discussions do not lead to a cleansing of the heart, they only clutter <note>original: "incumber"</note> the head and spoil <note>original: "cloy"</note> the appetite. In this way, people treat truths the way children treat birds: they either crush them or let them fly away.

But even though the glory of the **second principle** <term>second principle: in Böhme’s theology, this is the realm of light, love, and the Son of God.</term> is so far beyond the reach of fallen humanity, it still has such an effect on the whole of creation that it can be proven to any rational person. As it says in Acts 14:17, "God has not left himself without a witness, by doing good, and giving us rain from heaven and fruitful seasons," etc.

### 1st demonstration of the second principle.

Although no language can express it, everyone who is regenerated <term>regenerated: spiritually reborn or renewed through divine grace.</term> knows this, in proportion to the degree of regeneration they have attained, for they live in it. And the more or less such people are "sick" with sacred love <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 2:5; it describes being overwhelmed or fainting with intense spiritual longing for God.</note>, the more or less they feel this. The more we die to our immoderate lusts for dominion over the whole **third principle** <term>third principle: the physical, visible world.</term>, the more this principle reveals itself in us. 

And in the same proportion that we enlighten our **first principle**—which is the soul's original state within the first four forms of nature <term>four forms of nature: in Böhme’s complex cosmology, these are the first stages of spiritual manifestation (desire, mobility, anguish, and fire) that constitute the "dark" root of the soul before it is transformed by light.</term>—by using divine power to kindle the true light within them, our root is transplanted to grow in this paradisiacal <note>original: "paradisical"</note> field or principle.

-># Of JACOB BEHMEN'S Writings.<-

Nor should even Plato's description of love be considered inconsistent with this; he defines love as one's dying to their own body and living in the body of the beloved.

For living within the divine light and love of the **second principle** (which is the quality of the Son of God) is a dying to ourselves and our own interests. That is, it is a death to our entire depraved image, much like the way the day is the death of the night.

### 3rd demonstration of the second principle.

The **second principle** <term>second principle: in Böhme’s system, this represents the world of light, grace, and divine love, as opposed to the "dark" first principle of raw power or the "physical" third principle.</term> is demonstrated by the sacrifices that were consumed by the holy fire. Through this process, the offerings died to their original form and lived in a new one—that is, they were transmuted into the holy flame.

And just as that part of the sacrifice which would not catch fire in the holy flame (representing this principle) fell away as ashes into the perishing **third principle** <term>third principle: the temporary, material, and visible world.</term>, we see the same in the regenerate man. Much of the person who is being spiritually reborn remains "below" and is incapable of taking on the new divine image, remaining instead like ashes.

Now we see that ashes may be heated, but they do not burst into flame. Such is the earthly person and their sensual nature. Yet we see that after the process of a "second work" <note>likely referring to a further stage of spiritual alchemy or sanctification where the physical or "earthly" nature is further refined.</note>, people do transmute completely—

These ideas and beams of light, when received by the understanding from the second principle <term>second principle: the light of divine love and wisdom that enlightens the mind.</term>, are offered to the judgment to be considered.

# Considerations on the Scope

The passions are like furious, violent, and impetuous storms. They often cloud and deceive the eye of the mind and sometimes sink the ship of the whole person, setting the entire framework of nature on fire and into extreme distress, so that the judgment lacks the power to direct the will correctly.

Here the "new man" <term>new man: the spiritual self born of divine light, as opposed to the fallen, worldly self.</term>, as a child of the holy second principle <term>second principle: the light of divine love and wisdom that enlightens the mind.</term>, has great need to pray fervently at all times. He must know how to wait, practice much self-denial <note>original: "abstinence"</note>, and watch with the highest diligence. He must stand on guard and use great force and severity—often fighting a faithful battle—until he kills the "murderers" <note>the "murderers" refer to the sinful impulses or ego-driven desires that destroy spiritual life</note> that would destroy him. He must "die" to his selfish desires from day to day, so that he may be pulled like a burning branch out of the fire. He will find it absolutely necessary to have help from heaven to "bind the strong man" <note>A reference to Matthew 12:29, meaning to overcome the powerful influence of the ego or the fallen nature.</note>.

This is especially true for some people whose origins <note>original: "source or originals"</note> are strongly rooted in the first four forms of the first principle <term>first four forms: in Böhme's system, these are the harsh, contracting, and agonizing qualities of nature—desire, mobility, perception, and fire—that exist before the light of God breaks through.</term> passed down through birth <note>original: "generation"</note>. These qualities may also be reinforced by their constellation <note>their natural temperament or astrological disposition</note>, so that they have much of the "serpent" <note>the cunning, fallen, or malicious nature</note> within them. In such souls, the work of resignation <term>resignation: the act of surrendering one's own will to the divine will.</term> to divine guidance proceeds very difficultly, slowly, and sadly, with many

<header>On JACOB BÖHME'S <note>original: "Behmen's"</note> Writings.</header>

*Fourth demonstration of the second principle.*

Nature’s labor toward perfection, and the tincture <term>tincture: in Böhme's philosophy, a spiritualizing or transformative essence within nature that strives to elevate matter toward divine perfection.</term> within it, shows some faint impulses by producing precious stones, gold, and other metals, medicinal earths, spices, excellent fruits, and so on. And in the seas, it produces pearls, amber, and more. All of these are but shadows and reflected figures with which the third principle <term>third principle: for Böhme, the visible, material world, which is a manifestation of the underlying spiritual principles.</term> represents the glories of the second principle <term>second principle: the divine world of light, love, and spiritual reality.</term>.

Therefore, enlightened judgment guides the will not to value these things for themselves, but for the infinite excellence of that which is represented by them.

If it is true that I do not love the part I see or touch of my worthy, honored friend, but rather love him for the part of him I cannot see or touch outwardly—for that is indeed his excellence and the true man—then I should even less value his mere shadow.

<sig>H 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: CHAP.</meta>

<summary>This page explains that the beauty and value found in nature—such as gold, gems, and fruits—are merely physical shadows reflecting the higher spiritual glory of the divine realm. It argues that a wise person values the spiritual reality behind these objects rather than the material objects themselves.</summary>

# CHAPTER XIII.

## Concerning the third principle in four
## demonstrations.

If the Greek Christians think the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note> proceeds only from the Father, and not from the Son, they must probably have some meaning other than what is known to some of us; for the Holy Spirit proceeds from both the Father and the Son. <note>This refers to the "Filioque" controversy, a major theological division between the Eastern Orthodox and Western churches regarding whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone or from both the Father and the Son.</note>

The four forms of the first principle generate the light of the second, and from the infinite might and strength of the first, and the infinite radiance <note>original: "lustre"</note> of the second, the infinite glory and love of the third is generated. This third principle is the characteristic <note>original: "property"</note> of the third Person in the Trinity.

Because of this, the third principle is not only the growing power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of Paradise, but also of the external manifestation <term>out-birth: in Böhme’s philosophy, the "out-birth" is the physical universe, which is "born out" of the spiritual realm as a tangible reflection of divine principles.</term>; it encompasses the astral, elementary, and visible material world as well.

<meta>catchword: 1st</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the "third principle" in Jacob Böhme's thought, identifying it with the Holy Spirit and the creation of the physical, material world. It argues that the material universe is a tangible manifestation of the interaction between the divine principles of power and light.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Holy Spirit, Trinity, third principle, out-birth, Filioque controversy, Greek Christians, cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "third principle" from Chapter 13. It presents the first of four demonstrations aimed at proving that the physical universe (the out-birth) corresponds to the third Person of the Trinity (the Holy Spirit).</meta>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>Of Jacob Böhme's <note>original: "Behmen's"</note> Writings.</header>

First demonstration of the third principle.

For just as the air is produced by fire and light, yet once produced, is not only a self-subsisting body but also the food of the fire—and consequently of the light—so is the third Person in the Trinity to the first and second.

The external manifestation <term>out-birth: in Böhme’s philosophy, the physical universe, which is "born out" of the spiritual realm as a tangible reflection of divine principles.</term> is not the *first* principle, for it cannot be a root, but rather a fruit; not a foundation, but a superstructure; not a creator, but a creation. It is composed of darkness or substantial matter—like a chaos arising from the first principle—and the light arising from the second.

Neither is it the *second* principle, as is apparent. It is a distinct life, in a way inanimate and without understanding, an image of the inward spiritual world in its various figures and properties, representing everything as if in a mirror. We see that the outward glorious sun symbolizes <note>original: "figures"</note> the infinite inward second principle, but it is not a *seeing* light, but a *seen* light or power.

What then, if it is neither the first nor the second, can it be but the third? and though it is only the third, yet it possesses in every part of it the strength <note>original: "vigour"</note> of the first principle,

<summary>This page provides the first demonstration of Böhme's third principle, arguing that the material world functions like "air" to "fire and light," acting as a reflection or "out-birth" of the divine Trinity. It distinguishes the physical world from the Father (the root) and the Son (the light), identifying it instead as the manifestation of the Holy Spirit.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Trinity, third principle, out-birth, first principle, second principle, Holy Spirit, cosmology, light and darkness</keywords>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>Considerations on the Scope</header>

principle, and the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of the second in some measure or another. For if in any part there was nothing of the first principle, there could be no being but only nothingness <note>original: "nullity"</note>; and if in any part there was nothing of the second, then only death and hell would be revealed <note>original: "manifest"</note>.

Now, because <note>original: "forasmuch as"</note> in some places and things the first principle swallows up the second, the wrath and curse seem to be the only things present there. But in other parts or things, the second or "sweet property" of life is dominant; there, a life arises, for his light is the life of humanity.

### Second demonstration of the third principle.

But in some cases, the love in the light also swallows up the first principle. In these, there is a twofold birth: one inward and one outward. The inward is divine, heavenly, and holy in its external excellence—as seen in the "new birth" or regeneration. However, this inward birth does not extend so far as to transform <term>tincture: in Böhme’s theology, this refers to the spiritual infusion or "dyeing" of a substance to change its fundamental nature.</term> the outward person, because the curse has subjected the physical body to wrath and corruption.

The Lord Jesus Christ broke these bonds, for it was not possible for him to be held <note>original: "holden"</note> by them, even though the weight of the whole world lay upon him.

### What the heavenly humanity is and does.

His heavenly humanity did not only unite itself to the mortal flesh of the Virgin Mary; this heavenly humanity was the "Holy Thing" begotten of the Holy Spirit, the pure virgin of modesty, the Virgin Sophia <note>original: "virgin Sophia"; in Böhme’s theology, Sophia represents Divine Wisdom, the eternal bride of the soul and the mirror of God's glory.</note>, the firstborn of every creature, the heir of all things, the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world, and the Word that was made flesh. Furthermore, the same divine Man—who both came from heaven into the Virgin's womb and yet remained in heaven—this very same Word united itself to the second person of the Trinity, the Almighty Son of the Almighty God. Thus, this Savior and Mediator, our Immanuel, is the mighty one on whom help was laid; for in him the three principles <note>Böhme’s system: 1) The Father’s wrath/fire, 2) The Son’s light/love, and 3) The physical world produced by the Holy Spirit.</note> show themselves in their several incomprehensible fountains.

### Third demonstration of the third principle.

The love in the light can also tincture <term>tincture: to spiritually infuse or "dye" a substance, changing its fundamental nature or quality.</term> the outward person <note>original: "outward man," referring to the physical body and worldly nature.</note> in the third principle. The outward person may, by the pressing of the inward nature into and through it with

A glimpse of this is seen in the faces of Moses and Stephen, and more fully in the translation <note>original: "translation"; in a biblical context, this refers to being taken up into heaven without experiencing physical death.</note> of Enoch and Elijah, but above all in the Lord's transfiguration and his conversation after his resurrection. But it is a very difficult and great work because the entire third principle <note>In Böhme’s system, the third principle is the physical, material world and the human body.</note> within us is estranged and dead to it, due to the very heavy fall <note>The Fall of Man in Genesis.</note>.

->Fourth demonstration of the third principle.<-

It may also be observed that the love in the light has its outward figures and impresses itself within this third principle to the outward senses:

1. As in the astral kingdom <note>The realm of the stars and celestial bodies, believed to influence the physical world.</note>, principally in the sun, Jupiter, and Venus.
2. In the air, in the harmony of the elements, giving delightful, sweet weather.
3. In the sentient creatures, such as doves, lambs, etc.
4. In the harmony of musical sounds and the voices of birds.
5. In plants, by most healing <note>original: "sanative"</note>, fragrant <note>original: "odoriferous"</note>, and excellent fruits, roots, and seeds.
6. In the intricate colors of flowers, and in the variations of light and shadow in the clouds.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### How the sacrifices show the three principles. <note>In Böhme’s system, the "three principles" represent the dark fire/power (1st), the light/love (2nd), and the created material world (3rd).</note>

The three grand sacrifices were: 1. The burnt offering. 2. The peace offering. 3. The trespass offering, or sin offering, which unfold the threefold mystery.

The first grand order of sacrifices was the burnt sacrifices, which consisted of four kinds (Leviticus 2): either 1. of the herd, 2. of the flock, 3. of birds, or 4. of the grain offering <note>original: "meat-offering"; in the 17th century, "meat" referred to food in general, and in the Bible, this specifically meant offerings of flour or grain.</note>. In the same way, the first principle shows itself in four—and only four—forms. But again, the burnt offering differed from the other two sacrifices in three things. First, it had to be only a male (Leviticus 1:3), whereas the others could just as well be...

The burnt offering, after being washed, was wholly offered with no part held back. This shows **two symbols** <note>original: "figures"; in this context, these are "types" or spiritual representations of a deeper reality.</note>.

The first represents the Father’s property <note>In Böhme’s system, the "Father's property" refers to the first principle: the source of all power and the "dark fire" from which the light of the Son and the world emerge.</note> as one entire essence, without dividing the female spiritual qualities <note>original: "tinctures"; in Böhme's alchemy-influenced thought, these are spiritual essences or qualities that give matter its specific nature.</note> from the male. Although it is cut into pieces (from which all multiplicity <note>The state of being many or various; the opposite of unity.</note> exists), it is still one essence that cannot be divided from itself. This was represented by the burnt sacrifice, the only whole offering made without reserve.

It also represents man before he had taken any steps toward his Fall; for he was then part of the "entire one." Though he consisted of many parts, he was still able to go up wholly to the Lord, just as the whole burnt offering did.

The second grand sacrifice was the peace offering, which differed from the burnt offering as was noted: 
1. It could be from a female (Leviticus 3:1, 2). 
2. It was restricted only to the herd and the flock; whereas both burnt and sin offerings could also consist of birds and grain offerings <note>original: "meat offering"; in the 17th century, "meat" referred to food in general. In the Bible, this specifically meant offerings of flour or grain.</note>. 
3. It differed from the other two in that its fat and inner parts were...

...the Lord's, and the rest the priest's; but it was holy, and not to be eaten by any legally unclean person.

The peace offering was the symbol <note>original: "figure"</note> of the "new man" <note>In Böhme’s theology, the "new man" is the regenerated spiritual self that emerges through Christ, contrasting with the "old man" or the fallen nature of Adam.</note>, in whom the lost image which the first Adam forfeited is revived. By this reunion, the fallen man becomes new. This renewed man is sanctified to be the priest's portion, but the other part—namely, the renewing image itself—is God's part. So, in the Book of Revelation it is said, "And has made us kings and priests," referring to these two images:

1. The image of God, or the renewing power.
2. The renewed part, which is the companion <note>original: "consort associate"</note> and servant, always surrendering itself to the will of that image of God, or renewer. In this way, the servant also becomes holy and may not be touched by any unclean person, will, or spirit.

The third major sacrifice was the sin or trespass offering, which differed from the first two:

1. Generally, it was to be only a bullock.
2. Specifically, different types of sacrifices were assigned <note>original: "appropriated"</note> to various kinds of sinners and occasions.
3. The location where it was to be sacrificed: the fat was offered at the altar for burnt offerings, but the carcass, hide, and dung...

## CHAPTER 15.

### Of Jehovah and the Creation.

Now, after considering the three principles <note>The "three principles" refer to Jakob Böhme’s complex metaphysical system consisting of the dark world (fire/source), the light world (divine love), and the outward visible world (nature).</note>, let us proceed to a brief summary investigation <note>original: "disquisition"</note>. This may nevertheless assist the willing mind in discovering, through all created things <note>original: "the creatures"</note>, some glimpse of the Almighty Majesty whose offspring we are—or rather, whose offspring we once were. In our fallen state <note>original: "depravity"</note>, we truly retain only faint reflections and nearly worn-out impressions of that beloved, beautiful image we once possessed.

A glimpse of the Most High in the face of His only Son is, without comparison, infinitely preferable to all the most lofty speculation and wisdom of man. For this is what we lost through the Fall, and the knowledge...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divine knowledge lost in the Fall, explaining how humanity can rediscover God through the "two principles" of His power and His Godhead as revealed in creation.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>

<header>Of JAKOB BÖHME’S <note>original: "Jacob Behmen"</note> Writings.</header>

...of that divine knowledge, which we are taught to hope for in our restoration through Jesus Christ.

And although it is difficult, it is still possible for all in whom the light has so enkindled love that they search for it as they would for hidden treasure.

And because we are like someone who is near-sighted <note>original: "purblind"</note>, unable to see things far off, we must seek understanding through things within our view. For this reason, the apostle says: “For since the creation of the world His invisible attributes are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made, even His eternal power and Godhead” (Romans 1:20).

By His “eternal power,” we understand—according to the <term>first principle</term> <note>In Böhme’s system, the first principle is the dark, fiery source of all existence, associated with God the Father’s justice and wrath.</note>—the unbreakable bond and the Father’s nature <note>original: "property"</note>, through which He “is a consuming fire” (Deuteronomy 4:24). This is the labor called “his strange work” (Isaiah 28:21) and His “coming out of His place” (Micah 1:3).

But by His “Godhead,” we understand—according to the <term>second principle</term> <note>The second principle in Böhme’s thought represents the light-world, the Son, and divine love.</note> or the Son’s nature <note>original: "property"</note>—the state in which He is <term>Jehovah</term> <note>The personal name of God in the Hebrew Bible, often used here to emphasize His relational and merciful character.</note>: gracious and merciful, long-suffering, and abundant in mercy and truth; He is mercy itself, forgiving iniquity, transgression, and sin.

Now, to know God really and truly in any measure is to know the Son of God. He is the regenerator who—through the cooperation of the Holy Spirit—cleanses the heart of everything that may be called "Self." He subjects the heart to the obedience of the holy, universal will, making it run with delight and joy in that path which is painful <note>original: "grievous"</note> to the <term>unmortified</term> <note>The "unmortified" part refers to the aspects of human nature that have not yet been "killed off" or disciplined by spiritual practice; the selfish or sinful nature.</note> part of our nature.

For once the first <term>four forms</term> <note>In Böhme’s complex cosmology, there are seven "forms" or "qualities" of nature. The first four are the harsh, bracing, and "dark" qualities (contraction, friction, etc.) that eventually give birth to the fifth form: Light and Love.</note> have, by the grace of God, generated the fifth—namely, love in the light—the soul, thus divinely <term>irradiated</term> <note>filled with light</note>, falls intensely "sick of love." <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 2:5, describing a soul overwhelmed by its longing for God.</note> Nothing can provide ease or contentment any longer except when the soul is following its Beloved, whether by waiting patiently for Him, or by doing and suffering whatever He wills it to obey and endure.

### A brief look at the distinct knowledge of God.

Now we must understand that the first, second, and third principles <note>The "Three Principles" are Böhme's way of describing the Trinity’s manifestation: the dark fire (Father), the light (Son), and the outgoing movement (Spirit).</note> have existed from eternity, according to the attributes <note>original: "properties"</note> of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. Each lives within the other, and all share one holy, free, and all-powerful <note>original: "omnipotent"</note> will. We must also know that the infinite abyss, where the seven spirits dwell eternally, and...

Within even the smallest imaginable circle dwells the entire Holy Trinity—an abyss or immeasurable height, depth, length, and breadth. No man or angel can count its measure or even conceive of its vastness. This is like the holy body of God, who is its sovereign creator and owner, serving as its soul and spirit.

This pure abyss is holy. It acts as a single holy element—the seventh element (which is the workshop <note>original: "laboratory"</note> for the other six). It contains all powers and virtues, from which heaven, angels, and all lower creations originate. Within it is the divine <term>saliter</term> <note>Böhme used this term to describe the divine "sap" or spiritual "earth-matter" of the heavenly world—the foundational substance of divine nature.</note> and all qualities in unbreakable friendship <note>original: "amity"</note> and perfect harmony. These qualities embrace each other in perfect equality, forming the mystery behind all various powers.

From the first two principles <note>The Father (representing power/fire) and the Son (representing light/love).</note> within that abyss, and through the proceeding <note>original: "exit"</note> of the Holy Spirit, the throne angels were created. They were made according to the various properties of the seven <term>fountain spirits</term> <note>In Böhme’s cosmology, these are the seven core qualities or "source-spirits" that generate all of existence (e.g., Sourness, Bitterness, Heat, Light, Sound, etc.).</note> and their respective hosts. They possess the great power of the first principle, glorified by the majestic light of the second principle, the Son of God.

However, Lucifer and his angels extinguished within themselves the glorious sweetness of the

Without the light of the second principle, these qualities are harsh and conflicting.</note>, which—without the second principle (namely, the light of love, the Son of God)—exist in eternal conflict <note>original: "enmity"</note>.

### Where the two active elements originated

Also, out of the holy element, the two active elements were created: fire, according to the nature <note>original: "property"</note> of the Father; and air, which contains light, according to the nature of the Son. From these two elements—after the qualities were divided in the dark abyss <note>original: "dark deep"</note> as a result of the fall of the angels—came mortal water and dead earth. In both of these, because of the dominance <note>original: "prevalency"</note> of harsh contraction <note>original: "astringency"</note>, rocks and stones were formed.

To stop that excessive hardening <note>original: "immoderate coagulation"</note>, the Word <note>The Logos or Christ</note> commanded light to appear, driving out Lucifer. At this, the holy angels rejoiced:

> "Where were you when the morning stars 
> sang together, and all the sons of God 
> shouted for joy?" Job 38:7; 

For at that time, the <term>out-birth</term> <note>Böhme’s term for the physical, visible world as a "birthing out" or manifestation of the internal spiritual world.</note> or <term>third principle</term> <note>The material universe, which follows the first principle (the Father/fire) and the second principle (the Son/light).</note> was established through the proceeding <note>original: "exit"</note> of the Holy Spirit.

Then also out of the holy element and the divided qualities, which Lucifer had thrown into a confused chaos or dark abyss...

### The Creation

Also, out of the holy element and the astral birth <note>The generation of the stars and heavenly influences.</note>, the four elements emerged; and from these elements came temporary <note>original: "transitory"</note> creatures. Some of these bore the form of the first four qualities <note>The "first four forms" are the harsh, conflicting qualities of nature (sourness, bitterness, anguish, and fire) when they are not tempered by divine light.</note> separated from the light—such as venomous creatures, toads, and electric rays <note>original: "torpedo's"; in this era, the torpedo fish (electric ray) was often used as a symbol of numbing or paralyzing evil.</note>—which originated from the quality of "anguish." Others, like scorpions, spiders, and basilisks, came from the quality of "fire"; for every internal property sought to manifest itself as a creature. Some creatures also represented the second principle <note>The realm of light and divine love.</note> in innocence, meekness, and love, such as lambs and doves.

Man was created as an image of God, intended to be more than just a second race of intelligent beings <note>original: "intellectuals"</note>; he was to be a complete reflection of all three principles. That is: his soul was drawn from the first principle (the world of dark power); his spirit from the second principle (the

# Considerations on the Scope

However, hell is an intolerable torment to angels and to men because they were created for a different state, in another principle, and for another purpose.

Man was created from all three principles: with the power <note>original: "potence"</note> of the first, the love-fire of the second, and the moving life of the third. I come now to the following most important points and inquiries:
1. What man was like before the Fall.
2. The stages by which he fell.

### 1. Concerning man’s state before his Fall.

God created man in, or according to, His own image, and it has already been shown how he was God’s image—specifically, he was an essence <note>original: "extract"</note> drawn from all the three principles. <note>In the theology of Jacob Boehme, the "three principles" refer to the three realms of existence: the dark fire-world (God's power), the light-world (God's love), and the physical world of nature. **Seventh argument.** The law regarding impurity <note>original: "uncleanness"</note> after childbirth shows how impure this method of reproduction <note>original: "propagation"</note> is. For a woman who gave birth to a male child was ritually unclean for seven days and had to continue for thirty-three days without touching any sacred <note>original: "hallowed"</note> thing or approaching the sanctuary. But if she had a female child, her legal uncleanness lasted for fourteen days, and she was not purified until sixty-six days had passed (*Leviticus 12:2, 4, 5*).

In contrast, seven days was sufficient for cleansing a leper or someone who had a bodily discharge <note>original: "issue"</note>; on the eighth day, those individuals could enter the tabernacle of the congregation as before (*Leviticus 14:9, 10*).

All of this demonstrates that human birth through a woman contains much more than mere physical contamination since the separation of the tinctures <term>tinctures</term> <note>In the philosophy of Jacob Böhme, "tinctures" are the spiritual essences or inner qualities—specifically the male and female principles—that were once united in the original human but became divided during the Fall.</note>. For while leprosy was a physical impurity of a high degree, the fact that the cleansing of the woman lasted nearly five times as long for a male child, and nearly ten times as long for a female child, is powerful evidence <note>original: "pregnant testimony"</note> that the impurity of this form of reproduction is exceedingly great. This is what prompted David’s confession:

> “*I was shaped in iniquity, and in sin did my mother conceive me,*”
> *Psalm 51:5.*

### Of a virgin reproduction <note>original: "propagation"</note>.

**Question.** Will anyone ask: could a virgin reproduction have possibly existed?

# Considerations on the Scope

**Answer.** The Lord speaks nothing in vain, and it was he who said, "God is able to raise up children for **Abraham** from these stones" <note>A reference to Matthew 3:9.</note>. But—

It is also answered that although the arguments above may claim the force of a logical demonstration to an enlightened mind, the senses often rebel against solid reason. Therefore, the senses shall be satisfied by what follows, as far as the high nature <note>original: "sublimity"</note> of the subject may allow itself to be seen and touched.

->**Proofs for the senses regarding a virgin**<-
->**reproduction.**<-

**First proof.** Light passes through glass with very little loss, and it can even enhance growth <note>original: "generation"</note> by concentrating its central and original properties through magnifying glasses <note>original: "burning glasses," which are lenses used to focus sunlight</note>, by which it can also destroy. We see light not only passing through crystal, but multiplying through its own motion.

And can anyone think that Adam’s pure body (while it was still capable of eternal life) had anything in it that would block his magical will <term>magical will</term> <note>In the philosophy of Jacob Böhme, "magical" refers to the spiritual, creative power of the soul to act upon matter or spirit by the power of desire and faith, rather than "magic" in the modern sense.</note> from its divine work? Or rather, did it not have more assisting power than thick, heavy glass or crystal?

### Demonstration the fifth.

We see an ability to reproduce <note>original: "propagation"</note> in the universal life-force <note>original: "sperm"</note> flowing through the channels <note>original: "conduct pipes"</note> of the three principles <term>three principles</term> <note>In Böhme’s philosophy, the three principles are the dark fire-world, the light-world, and the outward visible world.</note>. The eye observes this in shellfish, trees, flowers, and herbs, which have their various kinds in their respective seeds. In these, their male and female qualities <term>tinctures</term> are united—represented as fire and light acting as one. And though these last—the plant kingdom <note>original: "vegetative tribe"</note>—are more distant from the divine than sentient beings <note>original: "the sensitive"</note>, yet every one provides a strong testimony to illustrate and lead us toward the most noble form of life.

And should anyone think that man could suffer from any defect that might undermine <note>original: "impeach"</note> his perfection? His original state exempted him (had he persevered) and set him on high, far from the reach of even the slightest hint of mortality—such as sorrow, weariness, sickness, or sleep.

Should he not have been fully able to perform all that was required to perpetuate his race by communicating his heat and light just as the sun does? This would have occurred in pure modesty and the highest humble love, so that his divine offspring might have assisted in that holy, paradisiacal <note>original: "paradifical"</note> employment.

## CHAPTER XVIII.

### Regarding some of the steps by which Adam declined toward his heavy fall.

**I**t has been shown from Scripture, proved to enlightened reason, and demonstrated to the very senses—though they are now so dull—that man, having both tinctures <term>tinctures</term> <note>In the theology of Jacob Böhme (Behmen), "tinctures" refer to the two fundamental spiritual qualities or energies—often associated with the masculine/fire and feminine/light principles—that were perfectly balanced in the original human being.</note>, was a perfect male and female in one, and was therefore "very good."

It is also clear that man should have exerted his holy will and united it with his excellent power. But it is just as clear that he did not do so. Because of this neglect, it was said, “It is not good that man should be alone, but a suitable helper <note>original: "meet help"</note> should be made for him” (Genesis 2:18). Then God cast...

Was Adam good? Perfectly so: intended to walk with God like a god, in a heavenly or paradisiacal <note>original: "paradiſical"</note> state forever. Does he need a helper, receiving an addition by way of subtraction?

If anyone wishes to penetrate into this mystery, let it be said to them: Since Adam would not stir up both the tinctures <term>tinctures</term> <note>original: "tinctures"; in the philosophy of Jacob Behmen, these are the two fundamental spiritual energies (the masculine/fire and feminine/light) that were originally balanced in the first human.</note>, which were united in him with great power, but instead wanted to be like an angel and remain barren as they are—whereas he, as a center or fountain, should have streamed forth a new race—therefore the gracious Creator let him sleep like someone in a faint <note>original: "swoon"</note>. Then God divided from him the female tincture and the lesser part of his essence or strength, represented by a rib (which is part of the outer protection of the internal organs of life), and with it built a "she-man," or woman.

->Now we come to the stages <note>original: "degrees"</note> by which man declined.<-

The first step must have been his failure to exert, execute, and exercise the power with which his gracious Creator had invested him.

The second step was his sleep. Sleep or fainting <note>original: "swooning"</note> is death’s elder brother, a weakness <note>original: "debility"</note> to the movement of sensory creatures; it is, in a large measure, an inability to act. Every inclination toward it is an act of hostility against an eternal, perfect state. Noah’s sleep showed the pattern <note>original: "figure"</note> of Adam’s shameful sleep, and Lot’s sleep represented something even worse than shame as a consequence of Adam’s sleep; yet both were brought about by their decline into the third principle <term>third principle</term> <note>In Boehme's philosophy, the "third principle" refers to the visible, material world of time and space, which is a temporary and flawed manifestation compared to the spiritual realms.</note>, in opposition to which the prophet Daniel calls the angels "watchers."

The third step was his being divided. Dividing by the separation <note>original: "dissection"</note> of parts is a reduction <note>original: "diminution"</note>, and is therefore diametrically opposite to wholeness and unity. For if even the smallest divisible part is severed or cut off from any body—assuming it lacks the smallest degree of infinity—that body from which it is cut, no matter how immense it may be, is made smaller until the section is as truly and physically <note>original: "locally"</note> reunited as it was before it was separated.

But Eve’s being taken from Adam made her a distinct individual <term>numerical self</term> <note>original: "numerical self"; this refers to being a separate entity that can be counted independently, rather than being part of a unified whole.</note>. This was a physical <note>original: "local"</note> severing, making her as truly distinct from him as the female is from the male in other creatures. ## CHAPTER 19

->What the condition of fallen man was after the fall.<-

The second principle <note>In the theology of Jacob Boehme, the "second principle" represents the spiritual world of light, divine love, and the kingdom of God.</note>—which is the majestic, sweet, divine love, the gentle light, and the pure <note>original: "chaste"</note> virgin of God’s wisdom—was withdrawn, and man was left dead in relation to it.

His soul, although it had lost the divine life of pure, angelic, and modest love, was yet born from the fountain spirits <note>The "fountain spirits" are the seven fundamental properties of nature in Boehme’s system that provide the source of all life and movement.</note> and was therefore incapable of dissolving. Instead, it had to remain as its own stinging fire and its own anguish, without any possibility of sparking the light within itself.

These opposing forces <note>original: "contrarieties"</note> (acting like murderers) might well be those whom Cain was afraid of after he had killed his brother.

His body (like those of the other creatures in the third principle <note>The "third principle" is the visible, material world governed by the four elements and the stars.</note> to which it now belonged) became subjected to harmful astral influences and to the divided properties of the elements. And he might—like wild, destructive, and ravenous beasts, birds, and fish of prey—kill and feed on the tame animals, living in fear and hostile conflict <note>original: "enmity"</note> with his fellow predators who reflect the image of the dark world <note>In Jacob Boehme’s cosmology, the "dark world" is the first principle of existence—a realm of raw, fiery energy and harshness that lacks the light and love of the divine.</note>.

He might also—until his astral revolution <note>original: "astral revolution"; referring to the completion of a person's life cycle as determined by the alignment and movement of the stars and planets</note> or some other evil brought an ultimate end <note>original: "extream date and period"</note> to his cursed, dying life—have produced children like other animals. He now carried those physical organs (reminders of sin and shame) for reproduction; his offspring would have been devils internally and beasts externally, according to the third principle <note>The "third principle" refers to the visible, material world governed by the four elements and the stars, which Boehme viewed as a temporary mix of good and evil.</note>. 

Indeed, nothing would have prevented their bodies from being shaped into the likeness of various animals, matching the natures, desires, and qualities of their minds—such as dogs, goats, foxes, pigs, bears, wolves, tigers, vipers, serpents, toads, lions, and so on. They would have had no other resurrection than that of those harmful, hateful animals, reflecting the properties <note>original: "properties"; the specific spiritual characteristics or qualities that define a being's nature</note> of the dark world through their monstrous forms <note>original: "ideas"; in this context, the internal mental patterns or spiritual archetypes that dictate physical shape</note>.

Where fallen humanity was rushing can be seen further in some of the notorious consequences of our downfall <note>original: "defection"</note>.

In many regions, the Americans <note>At the time of writing, "Americans" referred to the Indigenous peoples of the Americas.</note>—like beasts of prey—devour raw flesh. Some, even worse, eat the flesh of serpents and other unclean creatures, and some are said to be cannibals. The dark world, or the "first principle" <note>In Boehme’s cosmology, the "first principle" is the raw, harsh, and fiery root of nature that exists apart from the light of God's love.</note>, has seized the Africans, shaping their very physical appearance with a dark complexion <note>original: "black hue"</note> to match what the author perceives as their fallen natures.

Similarly, the quality of astringency <note>In Boehme's system, "astringency" (or the "harshness") is a fundamental spiritual force characterized by contraction, coldness, and solidification.</note>—confirming the greedy <note>original: "covetous"</note> will of Lot’s wife—prevailed to a terrible extreme, so as to fix her as a pillar of salt.

In the same way, the same principles of bitterness, anguish, and fire consumed Sodom.

Such was the transformation of Nebuchadnezzar <note>Referencing the biblical King of Babylon who was driven mad and lived like an animal, as described in the Book of Daniel.</note> that his hair became like birds' feathers and his nails like claws.

Our physical body <note>original: "elementary structure"</note> has fallen into such a state of subjection since the Fall that it can be reshaped as easily as an unbaked <note>original: "unburnt"</note> clay vessel.

Humanity became vain in its imagination, and its foolish heart was darkened. People became worshippers of angels, of the stars in the sky <note>original: "hosts of heaven"</note>, of demons, of brute animals, and even of garlic <note>A reference to ancient Egyptian practices as described by Roman satirists, often cited by early modern writers to show the depths of idolatry.</note>.
